Asymptotics of the partition function for β \beta -ensembles at high temperature Thanks: This project has received funding from the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union Horizon 2020 research and innovation program (grant agreement No. 884584).
Charlie Dworaczek Guera

Abstract
We consider the real β \beta -ensemble (or 1D log-gas) of dimension N N in the high-temperature regime, i.e. where the inverse temperature β \beta scales as N ​ β = 2 ​ P N\beta=2P , with P P a fixed positive parameter. We establish the large- N N asymptotic expansion at all orders of the partition function: 𝒵 N [ V ] = ∫ ℝ N ∏ i < j N | x i − x j | 2 ​ P N ⋅ ∏ i = 1 N e − V ⁡ ( x i ) d x i \mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\prod_{i<j}^{N}\left|x_{i}-x_{j}\right|^{\frac{2P}{N}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-V(x_{i})}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{i} for V ⁡ ( x ) = x 2 + ϕ ⁡ ( x ) V(x)=x^{2}+\phi(x) with ϕ \phi a bounded smooth function, and identify the first two terms of this expansion. In this regime, the energy no longer dominates the entropy, as in the fixed- β \beta case, but rather scales at the same order in N N . Consequently, at large N N , the system is macroscopically described by the so-called thermal equilibrium measure which is supported on the entire real line. Our proof relies on the loop equations method, previously applied in the fixed- β \beta setting in [ 17 , 16 ] , and provides the first example in which this approach can be successfully implemented using the thermal equilibrium measure. This requires a detailed understanding of both the thermal equilibrium measure and the associated master operator, an unbounded differential operator, leading to several new analytical challenges. In this setting, we carry out a technically involved analysis to obtain precise estimates for the inverse of the master operator in suitable functional norms. In addition we establish, through subtle operator arguments, a crucial continuity property of the equilibrium density with respect to the potential dependence. These two results constitute the main novelties of the paper and allow us to exhibit a new class of multiple integrals for which such an expansion can be obtained, while providing a deeper understanding of the thermal equilibrium measure and its properties.



1
Introduction

1.1
Setting of the problem

Let
P>0
and
V:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a function growing sufficiently fast at infinity, see Assumptions
1.1
. The real
\beta
-ensemble of dimension
N
at high temperature is the particle system on
\mathbb{R}
,
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
with the following distribution:

\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{V}_{N}(\underline{x})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}p_{N}^{V}(\underline{x})\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\dots\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{N}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{ with }\hskip 14.22636ptp_{N}^{V}(\underline{x})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]}\prod_{i<j}^{N}\left|x_{i}-x_{j}\right|^{\frac{2P}{N}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-V(x_{i})}

(1)

where
\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]>0
is the partition function that ensures that
\mathbb{P}_{N}^{V}
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, namely:

\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\prod_{i<j}^{N}\left|x_{i}-x_{j}\right|^{\frac{2P}{N}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-V(x_{i})}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{i}.

(2)

Here, the factor
2
in the two-body interaction is irrelevant and just makes the equations look ”nicer”. The main goal of this article is to establish the existence of the large-
N
asymptotic expansion of the free energy
\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]
under some assumptions on
V
namely that there exists
(c_{i})_{i\geq-1}
such that for all
M\geq-1
:

\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]=\sum_{i=-1}^{M}\dfrac{c_{i}}{N^{i}}+O\left(N^{-(M+1)}\right)

(3)

Obtaining such large-
N
all order expansions for the partition functions of Gibbs measures like
\mathbb{P}_{N}
is interesting for several reasons. It translates into expansions for the volume of certain high-dimensional convex sets
[
29
, Corollary 1.9]
, allows one to define certain quantities in quantum field theory
[
15
,
26
]
or to obtain topological data of the system in the expansion. Indeed, the entropy of the equilibrium measure, the Lebesgue measure
[
43
, Corollary 1.1]
or the number of connected components
[
16
, Theorem 1.4]
of the support can be found in the coefficients in the expansion. Furthermore, obtaining the form of the expansion (namely in powers of
N^{-1}
here) is interesting and is still unknown in the case of Coulomb gases, see
[
57
, Section 2.3]
.

To do so, we rely on the analysis of a certain tower of equations indexed by integers
n\geq 1
called the
loop equations
, or sometimes Dyson-Schwinger equations/Ward identities. This technique, which we describe further below, was first introduced in
[
3
,
2
]
and later developed in
[
17
,
16
]
.

It is well known that the leading order of this type of integral is obtained by using large deviations arguments for the law of the empirical measure
\mu_{N}
:

\mu_{N}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}},

see for example
[
4
, Proof of Theorem 2.6.1]
for
\beta
-ensembles with
\beta
fixed,
[
43
, equation (1.9)]
in the Coulomb/Riesz case or
[
15
, (2.4.2)]
for a sinh-interaction. In the context of
\beta
-ensembles at high temperature, a large deviation principle (LDP) for the law of
\mu_{N}
was shown in
[
32
]
. The rate function is given by
\mathcal{E}_{V}-\inf\limits_{\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R})}\mathcal{E}_{V}(\mu)
where:

\mathcal{E}_{V}(\mu)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}V(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)-2P\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log|x-y|\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(y)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log\left(\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)}{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}\right)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x).

(4)

This functional can be shown to admit a unique minimizer
\mu_{V}
that we call the
equilibrium measure
and which is sometimes referred to as the
thermal equilibrium measure
in the context of Coulomb gases
[
6
,
7
,
57
,
53
]
. This minimizer is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and its density
\rho_{V}
, called the
equilibrium density
, is supported on the whole real line, see
[
36
, Lemma 3.2]
for a proof and
[
1
]
for plots of
\rho_{V}
. As a consequence of
[
32
,
36
]
, we have:

\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}\dfrac{1}{N}\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]=-\mathcal{E}_{V}(\mu_{V}).

As the minimizer of the functional
\mathcal{E}_{V}
, it is known that there exists a constant
\lambda_{V}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\rho_{V}
satisfies the following equation:

V(x)-2P\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|x-y|\rho_{V}(y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y+\log\rho_{V}(x)=\lambda_{V}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{}x-ae

(5)

(see
[
36
, Lemma 3.2]
). In (
5
), it is the term
\log\rho_{V}
that forces
\rho_{V}
to be supported on the whole real line, since the two other terms in the LHS remain locally bounded. This is a fundamental difference from the usual
\beta
-ensembles, where the equilibrium measure is compactly supported. This equation can be rewritten as

\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\quad\rho_{V}(x)=\exp\Big(-V(x)-2PU^{\rho_{V}}(x)+\lambda_{V}\Big),\hskip 19.91684ptU^{\rho_{V}}(x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}-\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|x-y|\rho_{V}(y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y.

(6)

One can observe that
U^{\rho_{V}}
behaves like
-\log|x|
at infinity, see
[
27
, Lemma 2.4]
. Hence, assuming that
V
grows fast enough at infinity, unlike the constant
\beta
case where the equilibrium measure is compactly supported, the equilibrium density
\rho_{V}
here, ”only” decays exponentially fast at infinity.
This measure was first studied in
[
1
]
with quadratic
V
, where it was shown to interpolate between the Gaussian measure and the semi-circle law, see Figure 2 in this article. In the case
V_{G}(x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}x^{2}/2
the equilibrium density
\rho_{V}
, which actually represents
Askey-Wimp-Kerov
distribution in that case
[
8
]
, can also be made explicit:

\rho_{V_{G}}(x)=\dfrac{e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\dfrac{1}{|\widehat{f}_{\alpha}(x)|^{2}},\hskip 56.9055pt\widehat{f}_{\alpha}(x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\sqrt{\dfrac{P}{\Gamma(P)}}\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}t^{P-1}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\mathrm{i}xt}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t.

The LDP
[
32
]
establishes that
\mu_{V}
is the almost-sure weak limit of
\mu_{N}
from which we can deduce that for a large class of test function
\phi
we have:

\braket{\phi}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}\underset{N\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,\hskip 28.45274pt\Delta\mu_{N}=\mu_{N}-\mu_{V}

(7)

where we defined for any function
f_{n}
depending on
n
real variables and (possibly random) signed measures on
\mathbb{R}

\nu_{i}
:

\Braket{f_{n}}_{\nu_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\nu_{n}}^{V}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{N}^{V}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\nu_{i}(x_{i})\right].

(8)

The analysis of loop equations allows one to generalize (
7
) for smooth test-functions
\phi
and general
V
; and obtain the whole asymptotic expansion of linear statistics up to order
M
for all
M\geq 0
:

\braket{\phi}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\dfrac{d_{i}^{V}(\phi)}{N^{i}}+O\left(N^{-(M+1)}\right).

(9)

This method (as will be explained in Subsection
1.5
) relies on fine properties of a certain operator
\Xi
, called the
master operator
, defined, for sufficiently smooth
\phi
, by:

\Xi[\phi]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\phi^{\prime}+\big(\log\rho_{V}\big)^{\prime}\phi+2P\left(\mathcal{H}[\phi\rho_{V}]-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}[\phi\rho_{V}](y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(y)\right)

(10)

where
\mathcal{H}
denotes the
Hilbert transform
, which is defined for general functions by

\mathcal{H}[f](x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\displaystyle\fint_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{f(y)}{y-x}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y

(11)

where
\fint
denotes the principal value integral. This operator was shown to be invertible in a certain functional space
[
27
, Theorem 6.9]
and one of the main steps to establish (
9
) is to show that
\Xi^{-1}
is continuous on the functional spaces
W_{n}^{p}(\mathbb{R})
and for all
n\geq 1
and
p\in\{2,\infty\}
, defined by:

W_{n}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),\,\partial_{1}^{a_{1}}\ldots\partial_{k}^{a_{k}}f\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{k}),\,\sum_{l=1}^{k}a_{l}\leq n\;\text{with }a_{l}\in\mathbb{N}\right\}.\hskip 11.38092pt

(12)

In the special case
p=2
, we use the notation
H^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}W_{n}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. These spaces (for general
p
) are endowed with the norm:

\|f\|_{W_{n}^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\max\left\{\|\partial_{1}^{a_{1}}\ldots\partial_{k}^{a_{k}}f\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{k})},\,a_{l}\in\mathbb{N}\;\text{with}\sum_{l=1}^{k}a_{l}\leq n\right\}.

(13)

Combining such continuity properties of
\Xi^{-1}
and basic concentrations arguments is actually enough to conclude the expansion (
9
) of linear statistics at all orders for regular enough test functions.
Now, specializing
V=V_{G,\phi}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}V_{G}+\phi
where
\phi
is such that (
9
) holds then setting
V_{G,\phi,t}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}V_{G}+t\phi
for all
t\in[0,1]

\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V_{G,\phi}]=\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V_{G}]-N\int_{0}^{1}\Braket{\phi}^{V_{G,\phi,t}}_{\mu_{N}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t.

(14)

Recalling that the expansion of
\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V_{G}]
is easy to obtain thanks to Mehta’s explicit formula (
87
), obtaining the expansion of the LHS in (
14
) boils down to obtaining the asymptotic expansion of the integral in the RHS. Inserting the expansion (
9
) inside the integral, assuming that every term as well as the remainder is
t
-integrable, one obtains the desired expansion (
3
). By looking at the expressions of the coefficients
d_{i}(\phi)^{V_{G,\phi,t}}
and the form of the remainder, it can be shown that the continuity of
t\mapsto\rho_{V_{G,\phi,t}}
in a sufficiently nice functional space is enough to justify the integrability of each term in (
9
) and conclude.

1.2
Assumptions

Although Theorem
1.4
is proved only for
V=V_{G,\phi}
with
\phi
smooth and bounded, most of our arguments extend to a general potential
V
. Unless stated otherwise, we impose the following assumptions on
V
throughout the paper:

Assumptions 1.1
.

The potential
V
satisfies:

(i)

V\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
,

(ii)

V(x)\underset{|x|\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}+\infty
and
|V^{\prime}(x)|\underset{|x|\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}+\infty
,

(iii)

The measure
\mu_{V}
satisfies the Poincaré inequality, i.e. there exists
C_{\mathrm{Poinc}}>0
(depending on
V
and
P
) such that for all
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{R})

\mathrm{Var}_{\mu_{V}}(f)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(f(x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(y)\right)^{2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(x)\leq C_{\mathrm{Poinc}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f^{\prime}(x)^{2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(x)\,.

(15)

(iv)

For all polynomial
Q\in\mathbb{R}[X]
and
\alpha>0
, all
p\geq 0
,
Q\left(V^{(p)}(x)\right)e^{-V(x)}=\underset{|x|\to\infty}{o}(x^{-\alpha})
.

(v)

The function
x\mapsto V^{\prime}(x)^{-2}
is integrable at infinity, and
\dfrac{V^{(k)}(x)}{V^{\prime}(x)}=\underset{|x|\rightarrow\infty}{O}(1)
for
k\geq 2
.

Assumption
(i)
is needed in order to analyze the loop equations. Obtaining the expansion of linear statistics up to
o(N^{-k})
requires controlling building blocks appearing in the
i
-th loop equation for each
i\in\llbracket 1,2k\rrbracket
. The further along these terms appear in the tower of equations, the more derivatives and iterations of
\Xi^{-1}
(
\Xi
being defined in (
10
)) they involve. Since the regularity of
\Xi^{-1}[\phi]
depends both on the regularity of
\phi
and on that of
V
, assuming
V\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
allows one to obtain the expansion (
9
) at all orders. It also ensures that
\rho_{V}
is smooth, which will be necessary to show the continuity of the different operators involved in the loop equations.

Assumption
(ii)
ensures that
\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]
is well-defined. Indeed, the assumption on
V^{\prime}
implies that
V
grows faster than linearly at infinity. A further consequence, by Lemma
2.1
, is that
\rho_{V}
decays exponentially fast at infinity. The condition that
V^{\prime}
goes to infinity is also needed to ensure that
\Xi^{-1}[\phi]^{(k)}(x)\underset{|x|\rightarrow\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
for all
k\geq 0
and for bounded smooth functions
\phi
.

Assumption
(iii)
, together with
(i)
and
(ii)
, implies that
\Xi
defined in (
10
), is invertible; see
[
27
, Theorem 6.9]
. The authors showed that this condition is not overly restrictive
[
27
, Remark 1.3, Proposition 2.6]
. Indeed, for any potential of the form
V=V_{\mathrm{conv}}+\phi
, where
V_{\mathrm{conv}}
is strictly convex outside of a compact set and
\phi
is bounded,
\mu_{V}
satisfies the Poincaré inequality.

Assumption
(iv)
ensures that the equilibrium density
\rho_{V}
and all of its derivatives decay exponentially fast at infinity.

Assumption
(v)
is used to prove that
\Xi^{-1}
is continuous with respect to the norms (
13
). Indeed, when differentiating
\Xi^{-1}[\phi]
, for
\phi
a smooth function, quantities behaving at infinity like
V^{(k)}(x)V^{\prime}(x)^{-1}
naturally arise. On the other hand, in our approach we need to integrate some functions that behave like
V^{\prime}(x)^{-2}
at infinity.

These conditions are satisfied, for example, for every
V
of the form:

x\mapsto a_{n}x^{2n}+\phi(x),\hskip 28.45274pt\text{or}\hskip 28.45274ptx\mapsto\dfrac{e^{\gamma x}+e^{-\gamma x}}{\alpha}

where
n>0,a_{n}>0,\phi^{(k)}\text{ bounded }\forall k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha>0,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
. On the other hand, a potential like
V(x)=e^{x^{2}}
violates assumption
(v)
, therefore it does not fit into our analysis.

1.3
Main results

In order to state the next result, we recall that
\Delta\mu_{N}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\mu_{N}-\mu_{V}
and that
H^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
denotes the
n
-th Sobolev space as defined in (
12
).

Theorem 1.2
(Asymptotic expansion of linear statistics).

Assume
V
satisfies assumptions
1.1
, then for all smooth functions
\phi\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
for
r>0
(depending on
K
) big enough, there exists a unique sequence
(b_{i})_{i\geq\lceil k/2\rceil}
depending on
V
,
\phi
and
P
such that for all
K\in\mathbb{N}
:

\boxed{\Braket{\phi}_{\otimes^{k}\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=\sum_{i=\lceil k/2\rceil}^{K}\dfrac{b_{i}}{N^{i}}+O\left(N^{-(K+1)}\right).}

Our goal is to establish the existence of an expansion for
\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V_{G,\phi}]
where
V_{G,\phi}(x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}x^{2}/2+\phi(x)
and
\phi\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\bigcap_{n\geq 1}H^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. As described in (
14
), the idea is to deduce this from an integration of the expansion of
\Braket{\phi}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V_{G,\phi,t}}
for all
t\in[0,1]
where
V_{G,\phi,t}(x)=x^{2}/2+t\phi(x).

Making this step rigorous requires the following continuity result with respect to
t
. We recall that
W_{n}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
-norm is given by
\|f\|_{W_{n}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{k})}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\underset{i\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}{\max}\|f^{(i)}\|_{\infty}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume
V
satisfies
1.1
then for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
and all
\phi\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we have:

\boxed{\|\rho_{V_{\phi,t}}-\rho_{V_{\phi,t^{\prime}}}\|_{W_{n}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\underset{t\rightarrow t^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}0}

where
V_{\phi,t}:x\mapsto V(x)+t\phi(x)
for any
t\in[0,1]
. Furthermore, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
t\mapsto\rho_{V_{\phi,t}}(x)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and satisfies the following integro-differential equation for all
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
:

\partial_{t}\rho_{V_{\phi,t}}(x)=\left(-\phi(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(s)\rho_{V_{\phi,t}}(s)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}s\right)\rho_{V_{\phi,t}}(x).

Theorem
1.2
together with Theorem
1.3
allow us, thanks to (
7.3
) to deduce the following which constitutes the main theorem of the present article.

Theorem 1.4
(Asymptotic expansion of the partition function).

Let
\phi\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, then there exists a unique sequence
(c_{i})_{i\geq 0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
depending on
\phi
and
P
, such that for all
K\in\mathbb{N}
:

\boxed{\dfrac{1}{N}\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}\left[V_{G,\phi}\right]=\sum_{i=0}^{K}\dfrac{c_{i}}{N^{i}}+O\left(N^{-(K+1)}\right).}

The leading term is given by
c_{0}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}-\mathcal{E}_{V_{G,\phi}}(\mu_{V_{G,\phi}})
i.e.:

c_{0}=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}V_{G,\phi}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{G,\phi}}(x)+P\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log|x-y|\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{G,\phi}}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{G,\phi}}(y)+\mathrm{Ent}\left[\mu_{V_{G,\phi}}\right].

where

\mathrm{Ent}\left[\mu_{V_{G,\phi}}\right]=-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log\left(\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{G,\phi}}(x)}{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x}\right)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{G,\phi}}(x).

The subleading term
c_{1}
is given in terms of
\Xi^{-1}_{t}
the inverse of the master operator (defined in (
10
)) associated with the potential
V_{G,\phi,t}
. It can be written as

c_{1}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}g_{1}(P)-P\int_{0}^{1}\left[\Braket{\widetilde{\Xi_{t}^{-1}}[\phi]^{\prime}}_{\mu_{V_{G,\phi,t}}}+\Braket{\Theta^{(2)}\circ\widetilde{\Xi_{t,1}^{-1}}\left[\partial_{2}\mathcal{D}\circ\widetilde{\Xi_{t}^{-1}}[\phi]\right]}_{\mu_{V_{G,\phi,t}}}\right]\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t.

(16)

where
g_{1}(P)
is given in (
89
).

Above,
\Theta^{(2)}
and
\mathcal{D}
are explicit operators given in Section
3
while

\Xi_{t}^{-1}[\phi](x)=\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V_{G,\phi,t}}(x)}\displaystyle\int_{x}^{+\infty}\mathcal{T}_{t}[\phi](y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V_{G,\phi,t}}(y)

where
\mathcal{T}_{t}
is an explicit kernel operator given in (
86
). Given an operator
\mathcal{S}
, the symbol
\mathcal{S}_{1}
is also defined in Sections
3
and
4
.

We emphasize that Theorem
1.4
cannot be deduced from
[
17
, Proposition 1.2]
. Indeed, taking
\beta=2P/N
and then the large
N
-limit is different from taking the large
N
expansion of
[
17
]
valid for fixed
\beta
and then inserting
\beta=2P/N
in the large
N
expansion. Also some key points of the proof of the mentioned result are different in the high-temperature regime (different scalings, support of the equilibrium measure, form of the master operator or also the
V
-dependence of the equilibrium density).

1.4
Connection with the literature and motivations

Comparison with the fixed-temperature regime

The fixed-temperature regime of
\beta
-ensembles is given by the following distribution:

\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}_{N}(\underline{x})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}q_{N}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}(\underline{x})\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\dots\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{N}\hskip 11.38092pt\text{ with }\hskip 11.38092ptq_{N}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}(\underline{x})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}[W]}\prod_{i<j}^{N}\left|x_{i}-x_{j}\right|^{\beta}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-\beta NW(x_{i})}.

Above
W:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function going to infinity sufficiently fast and
\beta>0
. Setting,
\beta=2P/N
and
V=2PW
, one recovers the distribution
\mathbb{P}_{N}
(
1
). Since in the latter case the parameter
\beta
, interpreted as the inverse temperature of the gas, is very small, it justifies why
\mathbb{P}_{N}
is referred to as the high-temperature regime.

A great deal is known about
\mathbb{Q}_{N}
, owing to two decades of extensive study. First, LDPs for the empirical measure and the largest particle have been established in
[
12
,
11
]
, central limit theorems were proven in
[
39
,
17
,
58
,
9
,
41
,
20
,
10
,
56
,
5
,
29
]
, a full asymptotic expansion of the partition function
[
30
,
17
,
16
]
and local laws
[
21
,
19
,
20
,
56
]
have been shown. For
\beta=2
and
W
polynomial, the asymptotic expansion of the partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}[W]
has the form

\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}[W]_{|\beta=2}=\sum_{g\geq 0}\dfrac{c_{g}}{N^{2g}}

where the previous equality has to be understood in the sense of an asymptotic expansion. The coefficients
\left(c_{g}\right)_{g\geq 0}
correspond to enumerations of maps and, more generally, the asymptotic expansion of
\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}[W]
gives information on the enumerations of graphs embedded in surfaces
[
49
,
45
]
.

One of the main differences between
\mathbb{P}_{N}
and
\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}_{N}
is that, asymptotically the particles are spread over the entire real line in the high-temperature regime (the support of
\mu_{V}
being
\mathbb{R}
) whereas the limiting measure
\mu_{(\mathrm{fixed})}^{W}
of
\mu_{N}
under
\mathbb{Q}^{(\mathrm{fixed})}_{N}
is typically compactly supported (the semi-circle law in the case of the quadratic potential). This fact makes truncation arguments, where one makes the approximation

\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{N}(y)\right]=\mathbb{E}_{N}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)\psi(y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{N}(y)\right]+\varepsilon_{N}\left(f,\psi\right)

where
f
is an unbounded function and
\psi
a smooth function whose support contains the asymptotic support, more difficult to implement in the setting of
\mathbb{P}_{N}
. It is one of the main obstacles to establishing Theorem
1.2
for general (unbounded) smooth functions
\phi
and thus obtain Theorem
1.4
for general smooth potential
V
.

About the master operator, establishing invertibility and controls (see
[
29
, Lemma 4.1]
for example) in the fixed-temperature regime,
i.e.
for:

\Xi^{(\mathrm{fixed})}[\phi](x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}-V^{\prime}(x)\phi(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{\phi(x)-\phi(y)}{x-y}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{(\mathrm{fixed})}^{W}(y)

is also much simpler compared to
\Xi
(defined in (
10
)). It relies on the explicit inversion formula due to Tricomi
[
63
]
. The inversion of
\Xi
, obtained in
[
27
]
, is considerably more involved requiring Hilbertian techniques. An explicit and tractable expression for
\Xi^{-1}
is much harder to obtain, owing to the additional differential term. Thus, controls in
W_{p}^{n}
-norms (defined in
13
) are much more difficult to obtain. These continuity-estimates for
\Xi^{-1}
are one of the key ingredients to make the loop equation analysis method work.

Finally, one of the main differences between the high and fixed-temperature regime is the integration step,
i.e.
to deduce Theorem
1.4
from Theorem
1.2
and (
14
). This requires a precise understanding of the
t
-dependence of the coefficients
b_{i}
as well as the remainder in Theorem
1.2
when considering
V_{t}=tV+(1-t)V_{G}
for a general potential
V
. It can be shown that showing a continuity-type result for the equilibrium density in the potential
V
dependence is enough to conclude. While this step is technically involved in the high-temperature regime, it is straightforward to show in the fixed-
\beta
case. This is due to the ”linear”-dependence of
\mu_{\mathrm{(fixed)}}^{W}
on
W
, namely: setting
W_{t}=tW_{1}+(1-t)W_{2}
with
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
chosen such that
\mu_{(\mathrm{fixed})}^{W_{i}}
share the same support, then:

\mu_{(\mathrm{fixed})}^{W_{t}}=t\mu_{(\mathrm{fixed})}^{W_{1}}+(1-t)\mu_{(\mathrm{fixed})}^{W_{2}}.

(17)

This is proven in
[
17
, Lemma 5.1]
and is due to the absence of the entropy in the energy functional that
\mu_{(\mathrm{fixed})}^{W}
minimizes, see
[
17
, Theorem 1.1]
. In the high-temperature regime, the analogue of (
17
) is not true anymore for
\rho_{V_{t}}
(as can be seen from the asymptotic behavior of
\rho_{V_{t}}
at infinity for example) and the
t
dependence is much more complicated to capture.

The high-temperature regime

The study of the high-temperature regime started with the pioneering works
[
22
,
14
,
1
]
. More recently, LDPs for the empirical measure
[
32
]
, and for the largest particle at high
[
28
]
and intermediary temperature
[
55
]
have been shown. Central limit theorems for linear statistics have been established in
[
37
,
50
,
27
,
47
]
at a great level of generality and the Poissonian limiting microscopic behaviors
[
13
,
54
,
50
,
51
,
42
,
52
]
were shown in this regime both in the bulk and at the edge. Finally, in
[
31
]
, the authors analyzed the loop equations to deduce the moments of the subdominant correction of the equilibrium measures corresponding to Gaussian, Laguerre and Jacobi ensembles.

Asymptotic analysis of multiple integrals

From the point of view of asymptotic analysis of multiple integrals,
\beta
-ensembles provide,
via
its partition function, a good non-trivial (with an interaction between the integration variables) example of a
N
-fold multiple integrals whose AE is known at all orders
[
17
,
16
]
at least in the case of a smooth potential
V
. This was later generalized in
[
18
]
, to allow for general analytic
p
-interaction with
p\geq 2
, between the integration variables. In the so-called varying weight situation and with a
sinh
-interaction, the same authors managed to obtain the asymptotics of the partition function up to
o(1)
in
[
15
]
. Thanks to the developd tools, the authors managed to give a check on the so-called Lukyanov’s conjecture in
[
26
]
. Generalizations of these results on contours in the complex plane were established in
[
24
,
35
]
. A motivation to extend the first AE result of
[
17
]
to more general domain of integration, interaction and scaling in
N
in front of the potential is to developp tools for the so-called
quantum separation of variables method
, see
[
15
, Section 1.5]
, an alternative to the
algebraic Bethe Ansatz
.
In the so-called Freud weight context,
i.e.
when
V=|.|^{p}
, the
\beta
-ensembles are related to the geometry of the so-called
Schatten-balls
. For small
p>0
, the potential
V
is very singular and most of the techniques break down. However, AE of the log-partition function up to
o(N)
were established in
[
25
,
29
,
59
]
. A certain low-temperature regime of
\beta
-ensembles (with slightly different conventions,
i.e.

W
scaled as
N\sqrt{\beta}W
instead of
\beta NW
) with
\beta\rightarrow\infty
has been studied in
[
23
]
. Theorem
1.4
completes the picture by filling the gaps needed to perform the loop equations method.

Link with integrable systems

The study of the
\beta
-ensembles at high-temperature has attracted a lot of attention recently since links were discovered with integrable systems, such as the famous
classical Toda chain

[
62
]
. The integrable structure of this system, namely the existence of a sufficient number of conserved quantities, can be established by the existence of a so-called
Lax matrix
, whose spectrum is invariant under the dynamics. At long times, the model does not
thermalize
,
i.e.
it does not reach thermal equilibrium but is rather described by a more sophisticated probability measure called the
Generalized Gibbs Ensemble
(GGE)
[
38
]
. This is due to the existence of a set of locally conserved quantities, which highly constrains the dynamics. In the context of the Toda chain, the GGE has been studied in
[
60
]
and a link was established with the Gaussian
\beta
-ensembles. In the case of a Gaussian potential, it was shown that the distribution of the Lax matrix under the GGE was similar to the law of the tridiagonal representation of the Gaussian
\beta
-ensembles of Dumitriu and Edelman. This link was explored in
[
36
,
46
]
for more general potentials via large deviation techniques. Theorem
1.4
can be seen as a toy model to study more involved integrals appearing in this literature such as the partition function of the GGE of the Toda and Calogero fluid, see
[
61
, (9.34), (11.51)]
:

\mathcal{Z}_{N}^{\mathrm{sys}}[V]=\dfrac{2^{N}}{N!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}^{N}\underline{\lambda}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}e^{-V(\lambda_{i})}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{N}K_{0}\left(e^{-N\nu}Y_{j}^{\mathrm{sys}}(\underline{\lambda})\right)

where the zero-order Bessel function of the second kind is given by
K_{0}(x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-x\cosh t}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t
and

Y_{j}^{\mathrm{sys}}(\underline{\lambda})=\begin{cases}\prod_{i\neq j}^{N}|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|^{-1}&\quad\text{if sys}=\mathrm{Toda}\\
\prod_{i\neq j}^{N}\left(1+|\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}|^{-1/2}\right)^{2}&\quad\text{if sys}=\mathrm{Calogero}\end{cases}.

These integrals share many similarities with
\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]
in the sense that the leading order should also be given by a competition between the entropy and the energy and the minimizer,
i.e.
the equilibrium measure of both systems should also be very similar to
\mu_{V}
in our context.

The thermal equilibrium measure

The present article shows for the first time that the loop equation method can be used with the thermal equilibrium measure,
i.e.
the minimizer of
\mathcal{E}_{V}
. For general
\beta>0
(possibly depending on
N
), this measure (when adding a prefactor
\dfrac{2}{N\beta}
in front of the entropy in (
4
)) should provide a better description of the system than the usual equilibrium measure (when there is no entropy term in the functional). However, doing so implies dealing with a
N
-dependent measure and also master operator which is technically very demanding. The goal of this paper is to consider, the simplest case with
N\beta
constant. To the knowledge of the author, while this measure is well-understood in the context of Coulomb gases
[
7
]
, there is no good understanding in full generality of this measure quantitatively speaking in one-dimension.

1.5
Outline of the proof

The strategy used to show Theorem
1.4
is based on the following
interpolation equation
of the form:

\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V_{G,\phi}]=\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V_{G}]-N\int_{0}^{1}\Braket{\phi}^{V_{G,\phi,t}}_{\mu_{N}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t.

On the RHS, the expansion of
\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V_{G}]
follows from Mehta’s formula
[
48
, 17.6.7]
, see Theorem
88
. Once this identity is obtained, the expansion of the
free energy
of the model
\log\mathcal{Z}_{N}[V]
follows from the one for the second term in the RHS in the equation above. We now explain how to prove Theorem
1.2
,
i.e.
how to obtain the AE of
\Braket{\phi}_{\mu_{N}}^{V}
for general
V
satisfying
1.1
.

Expansion of linear statistics

The proof is based on the analysis of the loop equations. It consists of a tower of equations that link linear statistics of different orders. The simplest equation is the one at level
1
, which reads for any bounded smooth function
\phi
:

\Braket{\phi}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=\dfrac{P}{N}\Braket{\big(\Xi^{-1}[\phi]\big)^{\prime}}_{\mu_{V}}+\dfrac{P}{N}\Braket{\big(\Xi^{-1}[\phi]\big)^{\prime}}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}-P\Braket{\mathcal{D}\circ\Xi^{-1}[\phi]}_{\Delta\mu_{N}\otimes\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}

(18)

where
\mathcal{D}
is the operator defined for all
x\neq y
by
\mathcal{D}[\phi](x,y)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{\phi(x)-\phi(y)}{x-y}
. This equation links the
1
-linear statistic and the
2
-linear statistic
\Braket{\mathcal{D}\circ\Xi^{-1}[\phi]}_{\Delta\mu_{N}\otimes\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}
. Note that these equations have also been derived for the resolvent
W_{1}(x)
and two-point functions
W_{2}(x,y)
defined as Stieltjes transforms of
\mathbb{E}_{N}[\mu_{N}]
in
[
31
]
in the Gaussian case. For
W_{1}(x)
, the equation is asymptotically of Riccati type in the high-temperature, whereas it is merely quadratic in the fixed-temperature regime. The deduction of the asymptotic expansion for linear statistics from the loop equations is based on a so-called
a priori bound
, which we assume for the moment, of the following form:

|\braket{\phi}_{\overset{k}{\otimes}\Delta\mu_{N}}|\leq C\dfrac{\|\phi\|_{k}}{N^{k/2}}

(19)

for a norm
\|.\|_{k}
that we don’t make precise here. Such a bound also holds in the classical setting. Note however, that at high-temperature, one has to deal with a more complex norm which requires integrability conditions on the functions we apply this bound to. To explain how to obtain the first orders, we assume that we know that:

\Braket{\mathcal{D}\circ\Xi^{-1}[\phi]}_{\Delta\mu_{N}\otimes\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=\dfrac{\alpha_{1}(\phi)}{N}+o(N^{-1})\quad\quad\quad\text{ and }\quad\quad\quad\Braket{\psi}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=o(1),

for some
\alpha(\phi)\in\mathbb{R}
, then (
18
) allows one to obtain the leading order asymptotic for the 1-statistic:

\Braket{\phi}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=\dfrac{1}{N}\Big(P\Braket{\big(\Xi^{-1}[\phi]\big)^{\prime}}_{\mu_{V}}-\alpha_{1}(\phi)P+o(1)\Big)=:\dfrac{\gamma_{1}(\phi)}{N}+o\left(\dfrac{1}{N}\right).

Assuming now that for
n=2

\Braket{\mathcal{D}\circ\Xi^{-1}[\phi]}_{\Delta\mu_{N}\otimes\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{\alpha_{i}(\phi)}{N^{i}}+o(N^{-n}),

(20)

it is not hard to see that one can iteratively derive the expansion of
\Braket{\phi}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}
and get:

\Braket{\phi}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dfrac{\gamma_{i}(\phi)}{N^{i}}+o(N^{-n}),\hskip 56.9055pt\gamma_{2}(\phi)=P\gamma_{1}\left(\big(\Xi^{-1}[\phi]\big)^{\prime}\right)-P\alpha_{2}(\phi).

By the same procedure, one can see that the extraction of the asymptotic expansion up to order
n>2
of the
1
-linear statistics boils down to extracting the one for the
2
-linear statistics.

To achieve that, one needs to investigate the loop equation at level 2, which has the following form for a smooth function
\phi_{2}
of
2
variables:

\Braket{\phi_{2}}_{\Delta\mu_{N}\otimes\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=\dfrac{1}{N}\Braket{\mathcal{U}[\phi_{2}]}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}+\Braket{\mathcal{V}[\phi_{2}]}_{\overset{3}{\otimes}\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}+\dfrac{1}{N}\Braket{\mathcal{W}[\phi_{2}]}_{\Delta\mu_{N}\otimes\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}+\dfrac{1}{N}\Braket{\mathcal{Y}[\phi_{2}]}_{\mu_{V}}

where
\mathcal{U}
,
\mathcal{V}
,
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are some operators involving
\Xi^{-1}
in their definition. From estimate (
19
) that we assumed at the beginning, we know that:

\Braket{\mathcal{U}[\phi_{2}]}_{\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=O(N^{-1/2}),\hskip 28.45274pt\Braket{\mathcal{V}[\phi_{2}]}_{\overset{3}{\otimes}\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=O(N^{-3/2}),\hskip 28.45274pt\Braket{\mathcal{W}[\phi_{2}]}_{\Delta\mu_{N}\otimes\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}=O(N^{-1}).

It is straightforward to see that only the term
N^{-1}\Braket{\mathcal{Y}[\phi_{2}]}_{\mu_{V}}
yields a non-negligible contribution to the expansion of
\Braket{\phi_{2}}_{\Delta\mu_{N}\otimes\Delta\mu_{N}}^{V}
at precision
o(N^{-1})
. In order to push it up to
o(N^{-2})
, one needs to obtain the asymptotic expansion for the
3
-linear statistics and so on. Each additional order in the asymptotic expansion requires analysing a higher level loop equation. Crucially, only finitely many equations need to be analyzed at each order to capture all contributions, and the estimate allows one to neglect all the other terms and close the analysis. Finally, in order to apply the estimate to neglect the remainders, one needs to show that the operators involved in the SDE preserve enough of the regularity of the function they act on, especially for the inverse of the master operator
\Xi^{-1}
.

Controls on
\Xi^{-1}

In this setting, one has to obtain way more subtle controls compared to the constant
\beta
-setting. This is due to the fact that (
19
) involves a more complex norm than just a
L^{\infty}
-norm (which is the case for the fixed-temperature regime). Moreover, finding a manageable integral representation for
\Xi^{-1}[\phi]
in order to extract controls out of it, is a highly non-trivial step. This makes the proof of the continuity of
\phi\mapsto\Xi^{-1}[\phi]
quite technical. Finding such a representation and proving continuity estimates for
\Xi^{-1}
constitute one of the main technical contributions of this article.

Potential dependence of the equilibrium density

When integrating the asymptotic expansion of the
1
-linear statistics
\Braket{\phi}^{V_{G,\phi,t}}_{\mu_{N}}
, one needs to justify that the resulting integrals are finite,
i.e.
that the integrands are integrable. To do so, it is enough to show that the map
t\mapsto\rho_{V_{G,\phi,t}}
, referred to as the
perturbed thermal equilibrium measure
in the Coulomb gases literature
[
57
]
, is continuous with respect to the norm of uniform convergence of the function and all its derivatives. Establishing this in the high-temperature regime is non-trivial, due to the non-linearity of (
5
) in
V
. Theorem
1.3
provides a partial answer, in the case of the one-dimensional log gas, to a question raised in
[
57
]
concerning the perturbed thermal equilibrium measure. To show this result, our method is based on an application of the Banach fixed-point theorem to (
6
).

1.6
Notations and conventions

•

Let
X
be an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{k}(X)
(resp.
L^{p}(X)
) the space of functions differentiable
k
-times for which the
k
-th derivative is continuous (resp.
p^{\mathrm{th}}
-power integrable functions) on
X
.
\mathcal{C}_{c}^{k}(X)
denotes the space of functions of class
k
on
X
with compact support. For
p\in\llbracket 1,+\infty\rrbracket
, we denote by
L^{p}(X)
the usual Lebesgue spaces on
X
and by
L^{p}(\mu)
the Lebesgue spaces with respect to a Borel measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
. Furthermore, we define
L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
by
\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mu),\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu(x)=0\right\}
. For a function of several variables
f
, we denote the derivative operator with respect to its
i
-th variable by
\partial_{i}f
.

•

The space of functions
f
such that
f^{(k)}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
for all
k=0,\dots,n
is denoted
W_{n}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Its norm is classically
\|f\|_{W_{n}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\underset{k\in\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}{\max}\|f^{(k)}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}
.

•

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote by
\mathcal{H}[f]
the Hilbert transform of
f
defined by

\mathcal{H}[f](x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\fint_{\mathbb{R}}\dfrac{f(y)}{y-x}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y

where
\displaystyle\fint
stands for the Cauchy principal value integral.

•

We denote the Fourier transform of
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by

\mathcal{F}[f](t)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(x)e^{-\mathrm{i}tx}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x.

When
\mu
is a signed measure over
\mathbb{R}
, we shall denote its Fourier transform by the same symbol
\mathcal{F}[\mu]
.

•

The
1/2
-norm is defined for any function
f
which makes this quantity finite

\|f\|_{1/2}^{2}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|t|\left|\mathcal{F}[f](t)\right|^{2}dt.

•

We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
the set of probability measures over
\mathbb{R}
. For
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
we define the distance (possibly infinite)
D
by

D(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left(\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{t}\big|\mathcal{F}[\mu-\mu^{\prime}](t)\big|^{2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}.

(21)

•

We define the Sobolev spaces for all
m\geq 0
by

H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\|u\|_{H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}<+\infty\right\}

where

\|u\|_{H^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(1+\|t\|_{2}\right)^{2m}\left|\mathcal{F}[u](t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\right|^{2}d^{n}\underline{t}.

Above,
\|.\|_{2}
denotes the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
If
\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we also define

H^{k}(\mu)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mu),\,u^{(k)}\in L^{2}(\mu)\right\}.

The infinite order Sobolev space is given by
H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{p})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\cap_{k\geq 0}H^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
.

Outline of the paper.
In Section
2
, we establish an
a priori
bound on the
n
-linear statistics that is crucial in order to analyze the loop equations. To prove this bound, we first prove a concentration inequality for the empirical measure following
[
44
]
. In Section
3
, we establish controls on the operators that appear as building blocks of the loop equations. In Section
4
, we prove controls on the so-called master operator. These will play a crucial role in the analysis of the loop equations. We then state the loop equations and establish the large-
N
asymptotic expansion of the linear statistics, Theorem
1.2
, in Section
5
. In Section
6
, we establish the continuity of the equilibrium density in the interpolation parameter, Theorem
1.3
. Section
7
is dedicated to the expansion of the partition function and an explicit form for the free energy associated with the Gaussian potential, as well as the interpolation formula. We conclude with Theorem
1.4
. We detail in Appendix
A
some results obtained in
[
27
]
upon which this article largely relies. In Appendix
B
, we prove the continuity and the integrability of the constants that appear in our problem.

2
A priori bound on the linear statistics

As explained in the introduction, before analyzing the loop equations, one needs a bound that quantifies how small is a function integrated
n
times against the recentered empirical measure
\Delta\mu_{N}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\mu_{N}-\mu_{V}
. Before addressing this, let us recall certain properties enjoyed by
\mu_{V}
and the concentration results established in
[
27
]
.

2.1
Equilibrium measure

We recall the definition of the logarithmic potential (or sometimes called Symm’s operator)
U^{f}
of a function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. When it is defined, the latter is given for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
by

U^{f}(x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|x-y|f(y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y\,.

(22)

One can check that

\big(U^{f}\big)^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}[f]
.

We now describe the regularity of the equilibrium density
\rho_{V}
characterized by (
5
).

Lemma 2.1
.

[
27
, Lemma 2.2]

•

The support of
\mu_{V}
is
\mathbb{R}
and there exists a
P
-dependent constant
C_{V}
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\rho_{V}(x)\leq C_{V}(1+|x|)^{2P}e^{-V(x)}\,.

•

The density
\rho_{V}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and it holds

\rho_{V}\,^{\prime}=-\Big(V^{\prime}+2P\mathcal{H}[\rho_{V}]\Big)\rho_{V},

(23)

as well as

\rho_{V}\,^{\prime\prime}=\Big(-2P\mathcal{H}[\rho_{V}]^{\prime}-V^{\prime\prime}+V^{\prime 2}+4P^{2}\mathcal{H}[\rho_{V}]^{2}+4PV^{\prime}\mathcal{H}[\rho_{V}]\Big)\rho_{V}\,.

(24)

2.2
Concentration inequality

We now use an idea introduced by
[
44
]
and based on a comparison between a configuration
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})
sampled with
\mathbb{P}_{N}^{V}
and a regularized version
\underline{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{N})
, which we describe here.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and suppose (up to reordering) that
x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\ldots\leq x_{N}
. We define
\underline{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
by:

y_{1}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}x_{1}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{\text{ and }}\hskip 28.45274pt\forall 0\leq k\leq N-1,\;\;\;y_{k+1}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}y_{k}+\max\left\{x_{k+1}-x_{k},e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\right\}.

We denote
\mu_{N}^{(y)}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{1}{N}\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{y_{i}}
and also define
\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\mu_{N}^{(y)}\ast\mathcal{U}_{N}
the convolution between
\mu_{N}^{(y)}
and
\mathcal{U}_{N}
the uniform measure on
\left[0,N^{-2}e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\right]
.

Note that the configuration
\underline{y}
given by the previous definition satisfies
y_{k+1}-y_{k}\geq e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
, and
\underline{y}
is close to
\underline{x}
in the sense that

\sum_{k=1}^{N}|x_{k}-y_{k}|\leq N^{2}e^{-(\log N)^{2}}\,.

(25)

One can note that we have
|x_{k}-y_{k}|=y_{k}-x_{k}\leq(k-1)e^{-(\log N)^{2}}
, and we get (
25
) by summing these inequalities. As in the proof of
[
27
, Theorem 1.5]
, we obtain a bound on the density:

Theorem 2.3
.

For all
N\geq 1
and
\underline{x}=\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{N}\big)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,

p_{N}^{V}\big(\underline{x}\big)\leq\exp\Big(-NPD^{2}\left(\mu_{N,u}^{(y)},\mu_{V}\right)+K_{V}+2P(\log N)^{2}\Big)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{N}\rho_{V}(x_{i})

(26)

where
K_{V}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}2P\|\mathcal{H}[\rho_{V}]\|_{\infty}+C+P\displaystyle\Big|\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log|x-y|\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(y)\Big|
for some fixed,
V
-independent constant
C
and with
D
as given in (
21
).

Note that we have to keep the dependence on
V
in all of the constants involved in our problem.

2.3
A priori bound on linear statistics

Thanks to the bound given in Theorem
2.3
, we can prove the below
a priori
bound on the linear statistics. This bound is
a priori
in the sense that it is not optimal, namely, we will show later that for the
n
-linear statistics are
O(N^{-\lceil n/2\rceil})
versus
O(N^{-n(1-\varepsilon)/2})
as predicted by the
a priori
bound. Anyway, this will allow us to neglect, in the loop equations, the terms that are integrated with respect to
\bigotimes^{n}\Delta\mu_{N}
, where
\Delta\mu_{N}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\mu_{N}-\mu_{V}
. The following theorem and its proof are just adaptations of
[
15
, Corollary 3.1.10]
.

Proposition 2.4
(A priori bound on linear statistics).

Let
\varepsilon>0
, there exits
C>0
(depending only on
n
and
\varepsilon
) such that for all
f
in
W^{\infty}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap H^{n/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it holds

\boxed{\left|\Braket{f}_{\bigotimes^{n}\Delta\mu_{N}}\right|\leq\dfrac{Ce^{K_{V}}}{N^{\frac{n}{2}(1-\varepsilon)}}\left(\left\|f\right\|_{W_{1}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|f\|_{H^{n/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\right).}

where
K_{V}
is defined in Theorem
2.3
.

Proof
.

We use the decomposition
\Delta\mu_{N}=\Big(\mu_{N}-\mu^{(y)}_{N,u}\Big)+\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}
where
\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}=\mu^{(y)}_{N,u}-\mu_{V}
and obtain:

\Braket{f}_{\otimes^{n}\Delta\mu_{N}}=\sum_{l=0}^{n-1}\sum_{i_{1}<\dots<i_{l}}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{N}^{V}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})\prod_{j=1}^{l}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}(\xi_{i_{j}})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
\neq i_{1},\dots,i_{l}\end{subarray}}^{n}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\left(\mu_{N}-\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}\right)(\xi_{j})\right]\\
+\Braket{f}_{\otimes^{n}\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}}.

Since the
x_{i}
’s are not far from the
y_{i}
’s, we have the following bound by the mean value theorem and the fact that all the involved measures are probability measures:

\mathbb{E}_{N}^{V}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})\prod_{j=1}^{l}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}(\xi_{i_{j}})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
\neq i_{1},\dots,i_{l}\end{subarray}}^{n}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\left(\mu_{N}-\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}\right)(\xi_{j})\right]\leq\dfrac{C_{n}\left\|f\right\|_{W_{1}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}N}{e^{(\log N)^{2}}}

for some constant
C_{n}>0
only depending on
n
.

Let’s focus now on
\Braket{f}_{\otimes^{n}\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}}
. We know by Theorem
2.3
that

\mathbb{P}_{N}^{V}(\Omega_{N})=e^{K_{V}}O\left(e^{-cN^{\varepsilon}}\right)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{ where }\hskip 28.45274pt\Omega_{N}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{N},\,D^{2}\left[\mu_{N,u}^{(y)},\mu_{V}\right]>\dfrac{1}{N^{1-\varepsilon}}\right\}

for some
c>0
independent of
V
and for a remainder controlled
V
-independently. It ensures that:

\left|\Braket{f}_{\otimes^{n}\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}}\right|\leq Ce^{K_{V}}e^{-cN^{\varepsilon}}\left\|f\right\|_{W_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\mathfrak{R}_{N}[f]

where

\mathfrak{R}_{N}[f]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\mathbb{E}_{N}^{V}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{N}^{c}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}\,{}^{\otimes^{n}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})\right].

By Plancherel formula and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, one gets:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{N}[f]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{N}^{V}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{N}^{c}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathcal{F}\left[f\right](\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{F}\left[\Delta\mu_{N,u}^{(y)}\right](-\varphi_{j})\cdot\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}^{n}\underline{\varphi}}{(2\pi)^{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\mathcal{F}\left[f\right](\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})\right|^{2}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{n}|\varphi_{j}|\cdot\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}^{n}\underline{\varphi}}{(2\pi)^{n}}\right)^{1/2}.\mathbb{E}_{N}^{V}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{N}^{c}}2^{\frac{n}{2}}D^{n}\left[\mu_{N,u}^{(y)},\mu_{V}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\dfrac{2^{\frac{n}{2}}}{N^{\frac{n}{2}(1-\varepsilon)}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\mathcal{F}\left[f\right](\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n})\right|^{2}\cdot\left\{1+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\varphi_{j}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\right\}^{n}\cdot\dfrac{\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}^{n}\underline{\varphi}}{(2\pi)^{n}}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{n}{2}}\dfrac{\|f\|_{H^{n/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}}{N^{\frac{n}{2}(1-\varepsilon)}}

which concludes the proof.

\square

3
Controls of
\mathcal{D}

In this section, we will set some definitions of operators which arise as building blocks of the loop equations. After defining them, we will prove their continuity on appropriate spaces. This will ultimately allow us to apply the
a priori
bounds given in Proposition
2.4
.

3.1
Definitions

The operators that will appear in the loop equations at level
n\geq 2
are constructed via the following extension procedure, allowing one to extend operators acting on univariate functions into operators acting on multivariate functions.

Definition 3.1
(Extension of operators).

Given an operator
\mathcal{B}
that acts on functions of one variable and yields a function of
l\in\llbracket 1,2\rrbracket
variables,
\phi_{n}
a function of
n
variables, we define
\mathcal{B}_{1}
by:

\mathcal{B}_{1}[\phi_{n}](\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n+l-1})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\mathcal{B}\left[\phi_{n}(\cdot,\xi_{l+1},\dots,\xi_{n+l-1})\right](\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{l}).

(27)

For example
\mathcal{D}_{1}[\phi_{n}](x_{1},\dots,x_{n+1})=\mathcal{D}[\phi_{n}(\cdot,x_{3},\dots,x_{n+1})](x_{1},x_{2}).

3.2
Control on the non-commutative derivative operator

A first example of an operator appearing in the loop equations is the non-commutative derivative (NCD) operator.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, we define the NCD operator
\mathcal{D}[f]
by:

\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R},\,\mathcal{D}[f](x,y)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\begin{cases}\dfrac{f(x)-f(y)}{x-y}&\text{ if }x\neq y\\
f^{\prime}(x)&\text{ if }x=y\end{cases}.

In the following, we fix
p\geq 2
and show continuity results for the operator
\mathcal{D}_{1}
in the
H^{n}
and
W_{n}^{\infty}
-norms.

Theorem 3.3
(
H^{n}
-control for
\mathcal{D}_{1}
).

Let
n\geq 1
, there exists
C>0
(depending only on
n
) such that for all
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})\cap H^{n+1}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})
,

\boxed{\left\|\mathcal{D}_{1}[f]\right\|_{H^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{p})}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{n+1}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})}.}

Before showing this inequality, we need to show a general form of the derivatives of
\mathcal{D}_{1}[f]
.

Lemma 3.4
(General form for derivatives of
\mathcal{D}_{1}[f]
).

Let
\underline{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
satisfy
m_{1}\geq m_{2}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{p}m_{i}\leq n
. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{p}\in\mathbb{R}
be such that
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, then one has:

\partial^{\underline{m}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f](x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{j=0}^{m_{2}}C_{m_{1},m_{2},j}\dfrac{\left(g^{(j)}(x_{2})-\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m_{1}-j}\dfrac{g^{(k+j)}(x_{1})}{k!}(x_{2}-x_{1})^{k}\right)}{(x_{2}-x_{1})^{m_{1}+m_{2}+1-j}}

(28)

with
C_{m_{1},m_{2},j}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\binom{m_{2}}{j}(m_{1}+m_{2}-j)!(-1)^{m_{2}-j}
and
g=\partial_{2}^{m_{3}}\dots\partial_{p-1}^{m_{p}}f(.,x_{3},\dots,x_{p})
.

Proof
.

First, it is easy to verify that
\mathcal{D}_{1}[f]\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
for
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
. Secondly, when
n\geq 2
, by the Schwarz theorem, the order of the partial derivatives does not matter. It is only the derivatives with respect to
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
that are non-trivial to compute. Indeed, let
\underline{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be such that
x_{1}\neq x_{2}
, then

\partial_{3}^{m_{3}}\dots\partial_{p}^{m_{p}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f](x_{1},\dots,x_{p})=\dfrac{g(x_{1})-g(x_{2})}{x_{1}-x_{2}}

with
g\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\partial_{2}^{m_{3}}\dots\partial_{p}^{m_{p}}f(.,x_{3},\dots,x_{p})
. By applying the Leibniz formula when differentiating
m_{1}
times with respect to
x_{1}
, one gets:

\partial_{x_{1}}^{m_{1}}\partial_{x_{3}}^{m_{3}}\dots\partial_{x_{p}}^{m_{p}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f](x_{1},\dots,x_{p})=\dfrac{m_{1}!}{(x_{2}-x_{1})^{m_{1}+1}}\left(g(x_{2})-\sum_{k=0}^{m_{1}}\dfrac{g^{(k)}(x_{1})}{k!}(x_{2}-x_{1})^{k}\right).

Again, we differentiate
m_{2}
times with respect to
x_{2}
and apply the Leibniz formula to get (
28
).

\square

Proof
.

[of Theorem
3.3
]

Let
\underline{m}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}(m_{1},\dots,m_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}
be such that
m\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{p}m_{i}\leq n
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
m_{1}\geq m_{2}
. Let’s show that

\left\|\partial_{1}^{m_{1}}\partial_{2}^{m_{2}}\dots\partial_{p}^{m_{p}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f]\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p})}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{m+1}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})}

with
C>0
independent of
f
. The idea is to prove separately the
L^{2}
control on
\partial^{\underline{m}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f]
close to the singularity (the diagonal) and far from it. To do so, we will use the Taylor formula with integral remainder to deal with the singularity and Lemma
3.4
when we are at a fixed distance from the diagonal.

Close to the diagonal:
We first show this inequality on the subspace
\left\{\underline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},|x_{1}-x_{2}|\leq 1\right\}
. First note that

\mathcal{D}_{1}[f](x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{p})=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{1}f\left(x_{1}+t(x_{2}-x_{1}),x_{3},\dots,x_{p}\right)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t

an so by differentiating under the integral sign and by Jensen’s inequality, we obtain:

\left|\partial_{1}^{m_{1}}\partial_{2}^{m_{2}}\dots\partial_{p}^{m_{p}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f](x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{p})\right|^{2}\\
\leq\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{2m_{1}}t^{2m_{2}}\partial_{1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}+1}\partial_{2}^{m_{3}}\dots\partial_{p-1}^{m_{p}}f\left(x_{1}+t(x_{2}-x_{1}),x_{3},\dots,x_{p}\right)^{2}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, by integrating with respect to
\underline{x}
, changing
x_{2}-x_{1}
into
\widetilde{x_{2}}
, and using Fubini, we get:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\partial_{1}^{m_{1}}\partial_{2}^{m_{2}}\dots\partial_{p}^{m_{p}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f](x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{p})\right|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{|x_{2}-x_{1}|<1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}^{n}x\\
\leq\int_{0}^{1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t(1-t)^{2m_{1}}t^{2m_{2}}\int_{-1}^{1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{x_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{3}\dots\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{p}\partial_{1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}+1}\partial_{2}^{m_{3}}\dots\partial_{p-1}^{m_{p}}f(x_{1}+t\widetilde{x_{2}},x_{3},\dots,x_{p})^{2}\\
\leq C\|\partial_{1}^{m_{1}+m_{2}+1}\partial_{2}^{m_{3}}\dots\partial_{p-1}^{m_{p}}f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})}^{2}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{m+1}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})}^{2}.

Far from the diagonal:
Now we deal with the subset
\left\{\underline{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},|x_{1}-x_{2}|\geq 1\right\}
. By Jensen’s inequality and Lemma
3.4
, we get:

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{|x_{2}-x_{1}|>1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\left|\partial_{1}^{m_{1}}\partial_{2}^{m_{2}}\dots\partial_{p}^{m_{p}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f](x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{p})\right|^{2}\leq(m_{2}+1)\sum_{j=0}^{m_{2}}C_{m_{1},m_{2},j}^{2}(m_{1}-j+1)\\
\times\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{|x_{2}-x_{1}|>1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\dfrac{g^{(j)}(x_{2})^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m_{1}-j}\dfrac{g^{(k+j)}(x_{1})^{2}}{k!^{2}}(x_{2}-x_{1})^{2k}}{|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{2m_{1}+2m_{2}+2-2j}}.

with
g=\partial_{2}^{m_{3}}\dots\partial_{p-1}^{m_{p}}f(.,x_{3},\dots,x_{p})
. For all
j\in\llbracket 0,m_{2}\rrbracket
, the double integral in the last line can be estimated with another constant
C
depending only on
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
. For that, we use Fubini’s theorem, the fact that
m_{1}\geq m_{2}
: and obtain

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{|x_{2}-x_{1}|>1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\dfrac{g^{(j)}(x_{2})^{2}+\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{m_{1}-j}\dfrac{g^{(k+j)}(x_{1})^{2}}{k!^{2}}(x_{2}-x_{1})^{2k}}{|x_{2}-x_{1}|^{2m_{1}+2m_{2}+2-2j}}\leq\big\|g\big\|_{H^{m_{1}}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

Hence, after suming over
j
and changing the constant appropriately, we integrate over
x_{3},\dots,x_{p}
to obtain:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p-2}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{3}\dots\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{p}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\int_{|x_{2}-x_{1}|>1}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}x_{2}\left|\partial_{1}^{m_{1}}\partial_{2}^{m_{2}}\dots\partial_{p}^{m_{p}}\mathcal{D}_{1}[f](x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{p})\right|^{2}\\
\leq C\max_{l\in\llbracket 1,m_{1}\rrbracket}\left\|\partial_{1}^{l}\dots\partial_{p-1}^{m_{p}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})}^{2}\leq C\left\|f\right\|_{H^{m+1}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})}^{2}.

This is enough to conclude.

\square

Since in Proposition
2.4
, the bound on the linear statistic involves the
W^{\infty}_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
-norm, we state the following result.

Proposition 3.5
.

There exists a
C>0
such that for all
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})\cap W^{\infty}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})
,

\left\|\mathcal{D}_{1}[f]\right\|_{W^{\infty}_{n}(\mathbb{R}^{p})}\leq C(n)\|f\|_{W^{\infty}_{n+1}(\mathbb{R}^{p-1})}.

Proof
.

This follows from Lemma
3.4
together with the Taylor formula with integral remainder.

\square

4
Control on the master operator
\Xi

In this section, we study the so-called master operator which plays an essential role in the following. Indeed, proving continuity estimates for its inverse (which is well defined, see the following subsection) is a crucial step if one wants to analyze the loop equations. We first give all the definitions of the different operators involved in the study of
\Xi^{-1}
in Theorem
4.2
, then we show some appropriate decomposition for the derivative of
\Xi^{-1}[f]
in Theorem
4.10
and from that we finally conclude the continuity results needed to study the loop equations, namely the control in
H^{n}
-norms in Theorems
4.13
and
4.17
and in
W_{n}^{\infty}
-norms in Theorem
4.14
and Corollary
4.16
.

4.1
Definition

We recall the definition of the operator
\mathcal{L}
.

Definition 4.1
.

We define, for a sufficiently smooth function
f
, the operator

\mathcal{L}[f]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\Xi\big[f^{\prime}\big]=-\mathcal{A}[f]-2P\mathcal{W}[f]

where

\mathcal{A}[f]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}-\dfrac{\left(f^{\prime}\rho_{V}\right)^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}}\hskip 56.9055pt\text{ and }\hskip 56.9055pt\mathcal{W}[f]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}-\mathcal{H}\left[f^{\prime}\rho_{V}\right]+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}\left[f^{\prime}\rho_{V}\right](y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(y).

\mathcal{L}
is an unbounded operator on the space
\mathsf{H}
defined by:

\mathsf{H}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{u\in L^{2}\left(\mu_{V}\right)\ \Big|\ u^{\prime}\in L^{2}\left(\mu_{V}\right),\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(x)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(x)=0\right\},\qquad\Braket{u,v}_{\mathsf{H}}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\Braket{u^{\prime},v^{\prime}}_{L^{2}\left(\mu_{V}\right)}.

(29)

This space is indeed a Hilbert-space by the fact that
\mu_{V}
verifies the Poincaré inequality (see assumption

(iii)
). Its domain is defined by
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L})=\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{u\in\mathsf{H},\,\mathcal{A}[u]\in\mathsf{H}\right\}
by
[
27
, Proposition 6.8]
.
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{W}
are also unbounded operators on
\mathsf{H}
defined respectively on
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{W})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{u\in\mathsf{H},\,\mathcal{W}[u]\in\mathsf{H}\right\}
, see
[
27
, Section 6]
for a more detailed description of these operators. We also emphasize that
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{W})
by
[
27
, Remark 6.4]
.

For the next theorem, we recall that
\mathcal{A}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\rightarrow\mathsf{H}
is an invertible and diagonalizable operator with positive countable spectrum, see
[
27
, Proposition 6.3]
. We denote by
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{A})>0
its smallest eigenvalue. This quantity has a role in our problem since for all
f\in\mathsf{H}
,
\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\|_{\mathsf{H}}\leq\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{A})^{-1}\|f\|_{\mathsf{H}}
see
[
27
, Theorem 6.9]
.

Theorem 4.2
(Inversion of the master operator).

\Xi:\mathfrak{D}(\Xi)\longrightarrow\mathsf{H}
is invertible, of inverse defined for all
g\in\mathsf{H}
by:

\Xi^{-1}[g]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\big(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[g]\big)^{\prime}

where
\mathfrak{D}(\Xi)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}),\,\exists v\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{L}),\,f=v^{\prime}\right\}
. Furthermore for all
f\in\mathsf{H}
,

\left\|\Xi^{-1}[f]\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mu_{V}\right)}\leq C_{\mathcal{L}}\|f^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{V})}

(30)

where
C_{\mathcal{L}}\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{A})^{-1}
.

Proof
.

To prove that
\Xi
is invertible on
\mathfrak{D}(\Xi)
, the only thing to prove is that for all
v\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L})
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
which is true because by definition, for all
v\in\mathfrak{D}(\mathcal{A})
,
(v^{\prime}\rho_{V})^{\prime}=\rho_{V}\mathcal{A}[v]\in\rho_{V}\mathsf{H}\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
. This means that
v^{\prime}\rho_{V}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
and thus that
v\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Now it is obvious from the definition of
\mathcal{L}
and
\Xi^{-1}
that
\Xi\circ\Xi^{-1}=\mathsf{id}_{\mathsf{H}}
on
\mathfrak{D}(\Xi)
. The estimate comes from the fact that given
f\in\mathsf{H}
, one has
\Xi^{-1}[f]=\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}
. Then

\left\|\Xi^{-1}[f]\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mu_{V}\right)}=\left\|\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right\|_{\mathsf{H}}\leq C_{\mathcal{L}}\|f\|_{\mathsf{H}}.

\square

The crucial step when one wants to deduce Theorem
1.2
from the analysis of the loop equations, is to obtain controls on the master operator which we will show in this section. These bounds will allow us to apply the bound obtained in Proposition
2.4
to functions like
\Xi^{-1}[\phi]
.

4.2
Preliminaries

We define an operator
\mathcal{O}
whose iterations will appear in the derivatives of the inverse of the master operator (which exists because of Lemma
4.7
).

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be the operator defined on smooth enough functions by:

\mathcal{O}[f](x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left(\dfrac{f\rho_{V}}{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}\right)^{\prime}(x)

(31)

Remark 4.4
.

For example, with
\alpha\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{\rho_{V}}{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}
, it holds that:

•

\mathcal{O}[f]=\alpha^{\prime}f+\alpha f^{\prime}
.

•

\mathcal{O}^{2}[f]=(\alpha\alpha^{\prime})^{\prime}f+3\alpha\alpha^{\prime}f^{\prime}+\alpha^{2}f^{\prime\prime}
.

•

\mathcal{O}^{3}[f]=\left(\alpha\left(\alpha\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}f+\left(4\alpha^{2}\alpha^{\prime\prime}+7\alpha{\alpha^{\prime}}^{2}\right)f^{\prime}+\left(6\alpha^{\prime}\alpha^{2}\right)f^{\prime\prime}+\alpha^{3}f^{(3)}
.

In order to give a more precise description of
\mathcal{O}^{k}
, which will allow us to analyse its asymptotics at infinity, we need the following definition.

Definition 4.5
(Differential degree).

Let
f
be a function of one variable defined on
\mathbb{R}
differentiable
n
times, we define the differential degree denoted by
d_{\partial}^{f}
with respect to
f
by

d_{\partial}^{f}\left(\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{n}\left(f^{(i)}\right)^{\alpha_{i}}\right)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\sum_{i=0}^{n}i\alpha_{i}

For example the differential degree with respect to
f
of
\left(f^{\prime}\right)^{2}
and
f^{\prime\prime}f
is 2, while
d_{\partial}^{f}\left(\left(f^{(3)}\right)^{2}\right)=6
. Using the notion of differential degree, we are now able to state the next theorem.

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
k\geq 1
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{k}(\mathbb{R})
, there exists a family of polynomials
(P_{i}^{k})_{0\leq i\leq k}
such that

\boxed{\mathcal{O}^{k}[f]=\sum_{i=0}^{k}f^{(k-i)}P_{i}^{k}(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)}),\hskip 28.45274pt\mathrm{with}\hskip 28.45274pt\alpha\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{\rho_{V}}{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}}

(32)

In fact,
P_{i}^{k}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)}\right)
,
i\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket
, is the unique homogeneous polynomial in
i+1
variables, with differential degree with respect to
\alpha
equal to
i
, degree
k
and with coefficients independent of
V
satisfying the following reccurence relations:

•

P_{0}^{k+1}(\alpha)=\alpha P_{0}^{k}\left(\alpha\right)=\alpha^{k+1}

•

\forall i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,
P_{i}^{k+1}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)}\right)=\left(\alpha P_{i-1}^{k}(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i-1)})\right)^{\prime}+\alpha P_{i}^{k}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)}\right)

•

P_{k+1}^{k+1}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(k+1)}\right)=\left(\alpha P_{k}^{k}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(k)}\right)\right)^{\prime}=\left(\left(\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\right)^{\prime}\dots\alpha\right)^{\prime}

Proof
.

Let’s prove it by induction. For
k=1
,
\mathcal{O}[f]=\alpha^{\prime}f+\alpha f^{\prime}
and so by setting
P_{0}^{1}(\alpha)=\alpha
, which is homogeneous, of degree
1
and of differential degree
0
, and
P_{1}^{1}\left(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\right)=\alpha^{\prime}
which is of degree
1
and differential degree
1
, this proves the claim. Suppose that (
32
) holds at rank
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, then

\mathcal{O}^{k+1}[f]=\left(\alpha\mathcal{O}^{k}[f]\right)^{\prime}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}f^{(k-i)}\left[\alpha P_{i}^{k}(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)})\right]^{\prime}+\sum_{i=0}^{k}f^{(k-i+1)}\alpha P_{i}^{k}\big(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)}\big)\\
=\left[\alpha P_{k}^{k}\big(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(k)}\big)\right]^{\prime}f+\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}f^{(k-i)}\left\{\left[\alpha P_{i}^{k}\big(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)}\big)\right]^{\prime}+\alpha P_{i+1}^{k}\big(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i+1)}\big)\right\}\\
+\alpha P_{0}^{k}(\alpha)f^{(k+1)}

Hence by setting
P_{0}^{k+1}(\alpha)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left(\alpha P_{0}^{k}(\alpha)\right)^{\prime}
,
P_{k+1}^{k+1}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(k+1)}\right)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left(\alpha P_{k}^{k}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(k)}\right)\right)^{\prime}
and for all
i\in\llbracket 0,k-1\rrbracket
,
P_{i+1}^{k+1}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(j+1)}\right)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left(\alpha P_{i}^{k}(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)})\right)^{\prime}+\alpha P_{i}^{k}\left(\alpha,\dots,\alpha^{(i)}\right)
, we obtain the desired form of (
32
) and the recurrence relations. It remains to check that the homogeneity and degree conditions hold at rank
k+1
. This follows from the recurrence relations for the
P_{i}^{k}
’s.

\square

4.3
Closed form for
\Xi^{-1}

Before showing a closed form for the derivatives of
\Xi_{1}^{-1}[f]
and their
L^{2}
properties, we first prove that, if
f
is sufficiently smooth, these derivatives indeed exist in a strong sense.

Lemma 4.7
(Regularity of the inverse).

Let
f\in\mathsf{H}
such that
f\rho_{V}\in H^{n}(\mathbb{R})
with
n\geq 2
, then
\rho_{V}\Xi^{-1}[f]\in H^{n+1}(\mathbb{R})
. Furthermore if
f\in\mathsf{H}\cap\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
is such that
f\rho_{V}\in H^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, then
\Xi^{-1}[f]\in\mathcal{C}^{n+1}(\mathbb{R})
.

Note that the last condition is verified whenever
f
and its derivatives are continuous and grow slower than
e^{-V}
at infinity. The proof uses the operators
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{A}
introduced in Definition
4.1
.

Proof
.

We recall that
\Xi^{-1}[f]=\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}
. When
f\in\mathsf{H}
, we know that
\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. This is because
\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\in\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)
and
\left(\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}=\rho_{V}\mathcal{A}\left[\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right]\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.

We now want to show that
\left(\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\in H^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, let’s show first that
\mathcal{H}\left[\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right]\in H^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. First observe that

\rho_{V}\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]=-\rho_{V}f+2P\rho_{V}\mathcal{H}\left[\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right]-2P\rho_{V}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}\left[\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right](y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}y.

Hence, since
\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, so is
\mathcal{H}\left[\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right]
. The last term is a constant and clearly belongs to
H^{n}(\mathbb{R})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
hence
\rho_{V}\mathcal{A}\circ\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]=-\left(\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R})

and hence
\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{R})
. By bootstraping, this shows that
\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}=\Xi^{-1}[f]\in\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}}H^{n+1}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
by Sobolev-Hölder embedding theorem and hence that
\mathcal{H}\left[\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right]\in H^{n+1}(\mathbb{R})
. Since

\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime\prime}=f-\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}-2P\left(\mathcal{H}\left[\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right]-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}\left[\rho_{V}\left(\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}\right](y)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(y)\right)

(33)

and that
\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we can then conclude that, under the assumption that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
,
\left(\Xi^{-1}[f]\right)^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})
, hence
\Xi^{-1}[f]\in\mathcal{C}^{n+1}(\mathbb{R})
.

\square

The following lemma will also be useful for the controls on
\Xi^{-1}
as it is convenient to bound differently close to infinity and on a compact.

Lemma 4.8
.

There exists
M_{V}>0
such that for all
|x|\geq M_{V}
,
\Big|\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}}(x)\Big|\geq 1
.

Proof
.

From Lemma
A.2
,
\mathcal{H}[\rho_{V}]
is bounded and by assumption

(ii)
,
V^{\prime}(x)
goes to infinity at
\pm\infty
, the conclusion follows from the fact that
\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}}=-V^{\prime}-2P\mathcal{H}[\rho_{V}]
.

\square

We are now able to prove that a closed form holds for the derivatives of
\Xi^{-1}
.
The idea is to use the resolvant formula which gives that for all
f\in\mathsf{H}
,

\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]=-\mathcal{A}^{-1}\left[f+2P\mathcal{W}\circ\mathcal{L}^{-1}[f]\right]

(34)

and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{A}^{-1}[f](x)=\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}(x)}\int_{x}^{\pm\infty}f(t)\rho_{V}(t)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t.

(35)

It does not matter if one chooses
+\infty
or
-\infty
in (
35
) since
\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\rho_{V}(t)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t=0
but it will be convenient to make the choice
\mathrm{sgn}(x)\infty
for reasons that will appear further. We also mention that (
34
) is well-defined because
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{L})=\mathcal{D}\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{W})
.
Before establishing the continuity for
\Xi^{-1}
, we need to introduce an operator
\mathcal{X}
that takes a function in
\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}}H^{n}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{1}(\mu_{V})
and produces one belonging to
\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}}H^{n}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathsf{H}
by means of a recentering, where:

H^{1}(\mu_{V})\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left\{u\in L^{2}(\mu_{V}),\quad u^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mu_{V})\right\}

(36)

Definition 4.9
.

Let
\phi\in\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}}H^{n}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{1}(\mu_{V})
, we define the operator
\mathcal{X}
by

\mathcal{X}[\phi](\xi)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\phi(\xi)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(t)\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}\mu_{V}(t).

It is obvious to see that for all
\phi\in\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}}H^{n}(\mathbb{R})\cap H^{1}(\mu_{V})
,
\mathcal{X}[\phi]\in\mathsf{H}\cap\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}}H^{n}(\mathbb{R})
. We denote for all
u
such that
\mathcal{X}[u]\in\mathfrak{D}(\Xi)
and all
v
such that
\mathcal{X}[v]\in\mathsf{H}
,

\widetilde{\Xi}[u]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\Xi\circ\mathcal{X}[u],\hskip 56.9055pt\widetilde{\Xi^{-1}}[v]\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\Xi^{-1}\circ\mathcal{X}[v]

and, given a general operator
\mathcal{U}
, we adopt the notation
\widetilde{\mathcal{U}}
for the operator
\mathcal{U}\circ\mathcal{X}
when the latter is well-defined.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{n}(\mathbb{R})\cap\left(\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}}H^{n}(\mathbb{R})\right)\cap H^{1}(\mu_{V})
, for all
|x|>M_{V}
with
M_{V}
given in Lemma
4.8
, for all
k\in\llbracket 1,n+1\rrbracket
it holds that

\boxed{\left(\widetilde{\Xi^{-1}}[f]\right)^{(k)}=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}Q_{i}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(i)}\right)\beta_{k-i},\quad\quad\mathrm{where}\quad\theta\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}}}

(37)

and
\widetilde{\Xi^{-1}}[f]=\beta_{0}
.
The
\beta_{i}
’s are defined, for all
|x|>M_{V}
, for all
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, by:

\beta_{i}(x)\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\dfrac{-1}{\rho_{V}(x)}\int\limits_{x}^{\mathrm{sgn}(x)\infty}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t\rho_{V}(t)\mathcal{O}^{i}\left[\mathcal{X}[f]+2P\mathcal{W}\circ\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}[f]\right](t)

(where
\mathcal{O}
is defined in Defintion
4.3
). Above
Q_{i}^{k}
denotes the unique homogeneous polynomial in
i+1
variables with degree
k-i
, with differential degree with respect to
\theta
equal to
a
and with coefficients independent of
V
satisfying the following induction relations:

\displaystyle Q_{0}^{k+1}(\theta)=\theta Q_{0}^{k}(\theta)=\theta^{k}

(38)

\displaystyle\forall i\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket,Q_{i}^{k+1}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(a)}\right)=\theta Q_{i}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(a)}\right)+Q_{i-1}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(i)}\right)^{\prime}

(39)

\displaystyle Q_{k}^{k+1}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(k)}\right)=Q_{k-1}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(k-1)}\right)^{\prime}=\theta^{(k-1)}

(40)

Proof
.

We prove this statement by induction. For
k=0
, by (
34
) and (
35
), by setting

g\overset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}-\mathcal{X}[f]-2P\mathcal{W}\circ\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}[f],

by (
34
), we get for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\widetilde{\Xi^{-1}}[f](x)=\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}[f]\right)^{\prime}(x)=\left(\mathcal{A}^{-1}[g]\right)^{\prime}(x)=\dfrac{1}{\rho_{V}(x)}\int\limits_{x}^{\mathrm{sgn}(x)\infty}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t\rho_{V}(t)g(t)=\beta_{0}(x).

(41)

For
k=1
, differentiating again, which is allowed by Lemma
4.7
, we get for
|x|
large enough:

(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}[f])^{\prime\prime}(x)=(\mathcal{A}^{-1}[g])^{\prime\prime}(x)=-g(x)-\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}}(x)\left(\mathcal{A}^{-1}[g]\right)^{\prime}(x).

After integrating by parts in the last integral, we obtain:

\left(\widetilde{\Xi^{-1}}[f]\right)^{\prime}(x)=\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}[f]\right)^{\prime\prime}(x)=\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}^{2}}(x)\int\limits_{x}^{\mathrm{sgn}(x)\infty}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t\rho_{V}(t)\left(g\dfrac{\rho_{V}}{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}\right)^{\prime}(t)=(\theta\beta_{1})(x).

Since
Q_{0}^{1}(\theta)=\theta
, which is readily seen of degree
1
and of differential degree with respect to
\theta
equal to
0
.

Now, let
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
and suppose that (
37
) is true at rank
k
, then by differentiating we get:

\left(\widetilde{\Xi^{-1}}[f]\right)^{(k+1)}=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}Q_{i}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(a)}\right)\beta_{k-i}^{\prime}+Q_{i}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(i)}\right)^{\prime}\beta_{k-i}.

(42)

First, for
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
and
|x|\geq M_{V}
, we have:

\beta_{i}^{\prime}(x)=-\mathcal{O}^{i}[g](x)-\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}^{2}}(x)\int\limits_{x}^{\mathrm{sgn}(x)\infty}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t\rho_{V}(t)\mathcal{O}^{i}[g](t)=\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}^{2}}(x)\int\limits_{x}^{\mathrm{sgn}(x)\infty}\mathop{}\mathopen{}\mathrm{d}t\rho_{V}(t)\mathcal{O}^{i+1}[g](t)=(\theta\beta_{i+1})(x).

The second equality follows from an integration by parts and the fact that
\dfrac{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}{\rho_{V}^{2}}\rho_{V}\dfrac{\rho_{V}}{\rho_{V}^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}^{i}[g]
goes to zero at infinity. Hence (
42
) becomes

\left(\widetilde{\Xi^{-1}}[f]\right)^{(k+1)}=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\theta Q_{i}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(i)}\right)\beta_{k+1-i}+Q_{i}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(a)}\right)^{\prime}\beta_{k-i}\\
=\theta Q_{0}^{k}(\theta)\beta_{k+1}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(\theta Q_{i}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(a)}\right)+Q_{i-1}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(i-1)}\right)^{\prime}\right)\beta_{k+1-i}\\
+Q_{k-1}^{k}\left(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(k-1)}\right)^{\prime}\beta_{1}.

By the definitions of
(Q_{i}^{k+1})_{i}
, it is clear that (
37
) is true at rank
k+1
. The fact that
Q_{i}^{k+1}
are homogeneous and have degree
k-i
and differential degree
i
can be checked directly from the induction relations (
38
), (
39
), (
40
). This concludes the proof.

\square

Remark 4.11
.

When
V(x)=x^{m}
with
m
even, it can be checked from (
23
) that for every
i\in\llbracket 0,n-2\rrbracket
,
Q_{i}^{n}(\theta,\dots,\theta^{(i)})
is of the form
